The longest values in the file: a name of 381 characters, `remarks` of 689, `license_requirement` of 1,108 and `federal_register_notice` of 807; the most `alt_names` on one entry are 95, addresses 47 and `ids` 137. `start_date` and `end_date` are `YYYY-MM-DD`, except that one `start_date` is written `M/D/YYYY` with a leading blank. |
| Auth, limits | None. The file: at most 64 MiB (it is 34 MB), `application/json` and `application/octet-stream`, 180 s, a conditional GET on the stored `ETag` and `Last-Modified`, and a 304 is `unchanged`. The host after redirects must be `data.trade.gov` over https, for a 304 as well as a 200, or the sync fails as `wrong_root`; a body cut at the limit is `too_large`, and a request that fails is `fetch_failed`. `download` parses the text once and checks the root: an object whose `results` and `sources_used` are arrays, with `offset` 0 and `total` equal to the length of `results`, because a paged answer is not the whole list (`wrong_root`). It then projects: the entries that the rules of the lists below keep, reduced to the fields of the record and no others, sorted by list, compact name, start date and name, in one body, the canonical JSON of the versions (the `source_last_updated` of each kept list) and the entries. The `ETag` and `Last-Modified` it returns are the answer's, and `bytes` is the size of the raw file. The projection has two reasons: the file carries the time it was made (`search_performed_at`) and each list's `last_imported`, which are no fact of a list, so a hash of the raw file would change with them and the sync would never answer `unchanged`; and the 34 MB text and its objects are unreachable once `download` returns, before `parse` runs. `parse` reads the projection (the root is `consolidated.json`, or none when the body is not that shape), and the shrink check (20 percent) applies to its records. An entry of a kept list without a string name, with a name or other name of more than 500 characters, or with a field of the wrong JSON type, is left out and counted up to max(10, 1 percent of the entries read), as for the CVE records (Q7), and past that each is an error (`parse_errors`); an empty result is `empty`, and more than 20,000 records are not the list (`wrong_root`). |
| Cadence, increments | ITA's page says that its tools are updated automatically every day at 5:00 AM Eastern time, and each list has its own `source_last_updated`; the file is regenerated daily. Probed 1.5 hours apart on 2026-11-07 it kept its `ETag` and `Last-Modified`, so a 304 is the common answer to the check. The job runs every 6 hours at minute 7 (00:07, 06:07, 12:07 and 18:07 UTC, with the other sanctions lists). There is no delta file and no removal marker, so removals are found by comparing snapshots: an entry that the file no longer holds becomes `delisted`, and so does one that the rules below stop keeping, which relabels the pending findings that cite it in the existing way (D5.7 case 11). The version of a copy is the newest `source_last_updated` day among the kept lists. |
| Licence | **None published; accepted on 2026-11-07 (Q33).** The CSL is a compilation by ITA of lists that the Departments of Commerce, State and Treasury publish, a work of the US Government (17 U.S.C. §105), accepted in the way of Q5 and Q31 without a separate legal review. ITA's developer terms (developer.trade.gov, read on 2026-11-07 with a page reader) allow a service to search, display and analyze the data, name no licence, do not address commercial use, and reserve no rights. They ask (should, not must) for the notice "This product uses the International Trade Administration's Data API but is not endorsed or certified by the International Trade Administration.", and they bar modifying the content or misrepresenting it while naming ITA as its source. VerifyWise reads the downloadable file and not the Data API, so the attribution on the `vrdb_sources` row, which the Data sources line of a run shows, is the adapted sentence: `This product uses data from the International Trade Administration's Consolidated Screening List but is not endorsed or certified by the International Trade Administration. Entries adapted by VerifyWise.` Every record says that it is adapted and every cut text is marked `[shortened]`, so that no record passes for ITA's own words. The licence field reads `None published; a work of the US Government (17 U.S.C. §105) compiled by the International Trade Administration, whose developer terms allow services that search, display and analyze it; accepted on 2026-11-07 (Q33)`. The endorsement disclaimer page ([page](https://www.trade.gov/endorsement-disclaimer), read on 2026-11-07 with a page reader) is about ITA's own links and its use of trade names, and adds no restriction. The missing licence statement is the posture of OFAC (Q5) and of NHTSA (Q31): the terms grant display and analysis, and nothing reserves rights or limits commercial use, so there is no gate. If ITA ever objects, the copy is deleted and a gate is added; the notice that the terms ask for is shown, so that is the only realistic exposure. |
| Lists kept and dropped | The table below, by the code in the parentheses of `source`. **Dropped:** SDN, which `ofac` mirrors, and PLC (78 individuals, all legislators, no company). An entry whose code is not in the map is skipped and counted, and is no error, so a new list does not fail the sync, and a renamed large list fails through the shrink check. **Typed lists** (the OFAC ones) keep entries of type `Entity` only, as `ofac` does (a vendor is a company, and a person who shares its name is no reason to look twice): individuals, vessels and aircraft are dropped. **Untyped lists** (BIS and State) keep every entry, because a person cannot be told from a company there (that includes individuals, mostly on the Denied Persons List, who are stored with a name and a country only: D7, open item 3 after Q34). **Expired orders:** an entry of the Denied Persons List whose `end_date` is before the day of the sync (UTC) is dropped, and a later or missing end date is in force (1,204 of the 1,596 entries had expired on 2026-11-07). The projection of `download` applies it, with a clock that a test can set, and `parse` never does: the sync hashes the bodies and answers `unchanged` when the hash is that of the last success, so a filter that depends on the day has to change the bodies. **Known limit:** while ITA's file keeps its `ETag` the answer is a 304 and nothing is projected, so an order that expired since the last download stays in the copy until the file is regenerated, at most about a day. **The overlap with `ofac`:** an entry whose `entity_number` is also the `entity_number` of an SDN entry in the same file is dropped (90 entries of the Sectoral list on 2026-11-07, which `ofac` holds as SDN entries; 3 of the PLC's as well, which are dropped anyway), because `ofac` is the authority for them and two records would double one fact. On the file of 2026-11-07 the kept lists hold 5,380 entries, which group into 5,262 records, or 5,290 entries and 5,172 records once the overlap is dropped. |
| Record | One per list and name, not one per entry (`restricted_party`, a **state**: no lookback, and the record is offered as of the copy; `publishedAt` is the newest `start_date` of the group, or none, and a `start_date` that is neither an ISO day (`YYYY-MM-DD`, with a time or without) nor a trimmed month-first `M/D/YYYY` is no date and no error). A group is the entries of one list whose names are equal compared as letters and digits. That absorbs the repeats of the file (159 pairs of a list and a name occur more than once in the file: Entity List 86, Denied Persons List 68, ITAR Debarred 4, Nonproliferation 1; 101 remain once the ended Denied Persons orders are dropped, 10 of them on that list; one name with several orders) and does not rest on the file's own ids, which are hashes for the lists other than OFAC's (56 hexadecimal characters for four lists, 32 for two) and which nothing shows to be stable from one day to the next. The id of a record is the list's short id (the file's code in lower case, except `isn` for the Nonproliferation Sanctions and `nsmbs` for the Menu-Based list), a colon and the compact name of the entry cut at 160 characters (172 at most). The title is `Consolidated Screening List, <the list's short name>: <name>`. The text begins `US Consolidated Screening List (trade.gov).` and goes on with sentences, an empty field left out: the list (its full name from the map), the entry's name, the other names (the entry's `alt_names`, then the names of its `Issuer Name` ids: 6 at most and how many more), the countries of its addresses and of the entry (a two-letter value is upper-cased, any other is kept as the file writes it, cleaned and cut at 60 characters, with one spelling for each country; 6 at most and how many more), the programs, the websites (5 at most) and the registration numbers (6 at most) that the record is matched by, the day listed, for the Denied Persons List the day the denial order ends, the Federal Register notice (200 characters at most), the licence requirement and the licence policy (300 each), the remarks (400), `Entries of this name on the list: n.` when the group has more than one (the other fields and the match keys then come from the entry with the newest start date, and the entity numbers of the identifiers are those of the whole group), and the closing sentence: `The Consolidated Screening List is an aid to screening and not the official list: check the entry against the official publication of the list (the Federal Register for the lists of Commerce and State) before acting on it.` A cut ends in ` [shortened]`, at the last `;` or blank. The summary is at most 4,000 characters, enforced after it is assembled. The address is `https://www.trade.gov/consolidated-screening-list?list=<short id>&entry=<compact name>`: an entry has no page of its own, and the address is unique for each record, as NHTSA's and the FDA's are (the verifier maps an address to a record). `identifiers`: the list, its code and its official publication address (`source_list_url`), the entity numbers (OFAC lists), the programs, the countries, the websites and the registrations (type and number). |
| Left out and text safety | **Never stored:** the street, city, state and postal code of an address, dates and places of birth, citizenships and nationalities, gender, title, passport and national ID numbers, the vessel fields, the file's hashed ids, `standard_order`, `search_performed_at` and `last_imported`. **The remarks, the licence fields and the OFAC ids are free text that a model reads**, so each is cleaned (`cleanText`; compatibility forms are folded (NFKC), so that a full-width `<` is a `<` when the prompt's escaping sees it; zero-width and bidirectional control characters are stripped, as the ATLAS cleaner does; white space is collapsed; the few control characters U+0080 to U+009F that the file holds, which are windows-1252 bytes read as ISO 8859-1 such as the apostrophe in a country name, are read as windows-1252 and not dropped) and capped here, before the projection leaves `download`, and the text then goes through the `<evidence>` escaping and the untrusted-data line like every source. |
| Match | Names, weak: each of the name, the other names (100 read at most for an entry; the most in the kept entries is 28) and the names of its `Issuer Name` ids is a `name:` key, as written and without the legal form, of 4 characters or more, with the normalisation of `ofac`, at most 200 for a record. Websites, strong: the host of each `Website` id is a `domain:` key, strong against the vendor's verified domain, as the UK and EU lists' are (the OFAC lists carry 178 websites and 15 LEIs among the 5,380 entries kept, before the overlap is dropped). Identifiers: the ids of the types Legal Entity Number, Registration Number, Registration ID, Company Number, Business Registration Number, Public Registration Number, Unified Social Credit Code (USCC) and Tax ID No. give `lei:` or `regno:` keys: an LEI is strong alone and any other number only beside the name (Q1). Never a key: an ISIN, a SWIFT or BIC code, an equity ticker, a national ID number, a passport number, a date, gender, a title or an address. The BIS and State lists carry no website and no identifier, so a listing on one of them is always found by name: **a vendor that really is the listed entity gets Low and the name-only note** (D5.2). Keys are compared whole, so a vendor named "Acme Cloud" does not match a listed "Acme", and a name of fewer than 4 letters and digits is not a key. |
| Verdict | **Built, open (Q33).** The lists of Commerce and State are the ones that `ofac` does not cover, and the export control lists are where most of the AI vendors that matter are listed, so the list answers a question that the other sanctions lists cannot. A listing on the Entity List or the Military End User List means that an export licence is required for the entity, not that the entity is sanctioned; the text names the list, and the analysis prompt (`vr-14`) says so. The label for a real entity on those lists is Low with the note, by design: it says "an official list, identity not proven by a key", not "unreliable", and the finding's own text ("An entity named … is on …") and its action plan (check the address, the country and the owners) carry the weight; a High label for a collision on a short name would be worse. The most damaging false statement this change could produce is "the vendor is on the Entity List" from a name collision, and the Low label, the note, the wording of the prompt and the closing sentence of each record are there to prevent it. |

| Code | List (id) | Full name in the record | What is kept |
|---|---|---|---|
| `EL` | `el` | Entity List (Commerce, BIS) | every entry |
| `DPL` | `dpl` | Denied Persons List (Commerce, BIS) | every entry whose order is in force |
| `UVL` | `uvl` | Unverified List (Commerce, BIS) | every entry |
| `MEU` | `meu` | Military End User List (Commerce, BIS) | every entry |
| `DTC` | `dtc` | ITAR Debarred List (State, DDTC) | every entry |
| `ACN` | `isn` | Nonproliferation Sanctions (State) | every entry |
| `SSI` | `ssi` | Sectoral Sanctions Identifications List (Treasury, OFAC) | entities that are not SDN entries |
| `NS-MBS List` | `nsmbs` | Non-SDN Menu-Based Sanctions List (Treasury, OFAC) | entities that are not SDN entries |
| `CMIC` | `cmic` | Non-SDN Chinese Military-Industrial Complex Companies List (Treasury, OFAC) | entities that are not SDN entries |
| `CAP` | `cap` | Capta List (Treasury, OFAC) | entities that are not SDN entries |
| `FSE` | `fse` | Foreign Sanctions Evaders List (Treasury, OFAC) | entities that are not SDN entries (none today) |
| `SDN` | none | Specially Designated Nationals (Treasury, OFAC) | dropped: `ofac` mirrors it |
| `PLC` | none | Palestinian Legislative Council List (Treasury, OFAC) | dropped: individuals only |

### D2.20 FDA recalls of AI-enabled medical devices (openFDA)

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Operator | The US Food and Drug Administration, through openFDA, its public interface to FDA datasets ([documentation](https://open.fda.gov/apis/device/recall/)). A recall is a firm's correction or removal of a device, which the FDA classifies. The dataset has a row for each recalled product, and several products can share an event; it has no class field (Class I, II or III), which the FDA's device enforcement reports hold (read since 2026-11-10 for the class and the day of its classification only, Q37) and the FDA's recall page of the event shows (the page title reads "Class 2 Device Recall …" for a Class 2 recall). The recalling firm may be the maker's subsidiary or a distributor, and not the company that the FDA's list of AI-enabled devices names for the device. |
| Category · level | `ai_specific` · vendor, by name: the recalling firm, and the company of the AI-listed device that the recall covers. Class OFFICIAL, strength weak (D5.2). Only the recalls of devices that the FDA lists as AI-enabled (D2.17) are copied, joined by submission number, which is what makes the list AI-specific. It is the one keyless and official signal of adverse events for AI-specific products. |
| Endpoint, format | `https://api.fda.gov/device/recall.json`, keyless, JSON; the documentation allows 240 requests a minute and 1,000 a day for one IP address ([authentication](https://open.fda.gov/apis/authentication/)). Observed on 2026-11-07: updated weekly (`meta.last_updated` was `2026-11-05`); 59,703 recalled products, 38,912 of them with `k_numbers` and 2,776 with `pma_numbers`; an `ETag` with `Cache-Control: no-cache`; `limit=1000` is accepted; through the page guard with the bot's User-Agent the host answers 200. A search is `search=k_numbers:(K1+OR+K2+OR+K3)`: without the `+OR+` the parentheses mean AND and nothing matches, and a search with no match answers HTTP 404 with the error code `NOT_FOUND`. There is no field projection, and `code_info` (the lists of lots and serial numbers) is up to 3.5 million characters long, so the join of the probe that preceded the design, 314 recalled products, took 26 requests and 18,451,173 bytes of answers, the largest 4,098,164. A product has `cfres_id`, `product_res_number` (unique), `res_event_number`, `recalling_firm`, `firm_fei_number`, an address, `event_date_initiated`, `event_date_posted`, `event_date_created`, `event_date_terminated`, `recall_status`, `root_cause_description`, `reason_for_recall`, `product_description`, `product_code`, `product_quantity`, `distribution_pattern`, `action`, `code_info`, `k_numbers`, `pma_numbers`, `openfda` and `additional_info_contact` (a named contact and a phone number). Checked on 2026-11-07 over 400 products of four eras: `product_res_number` is `Z-NNNN-NN` (77 of the 100 products of 2002 to 2006) or `Z-NNNN-NNNN`, `res_event_number` is digits (five today), `event_date_initiated` is `YYYY-MM-DD`, and `recall_status` is `Terminated`, `Open, Classified` or `Completed`. **The class** comes from `https://api.fda.gov/device/enforcement.json`, openFDA's device enforcement reports, keyless and with the same limits and the same shape (`meta.last_updated`, `meta.results.total`). Observed on 2026-11-10: `meta.last_updated` was `2026-10-28` and the reports numbered 40,450; `limit=1000` is accepted; `Cache-Control: no-cache, no-store, must-revalidate`. A recall has one report, which is found by `recall_number` (the `product_res_number` of the recall dataset): `search=recall_number:(%22Z-1234-2020%22+OR+…)`, each number between quotes. A report is about 11 KB, so one search of 50 recall numbers answered 569,938 bytes, and a search that finds nothing answers HTTP 404 with the error code `NOT_FOUND`, as the recall endpoint does. A report has `recall_number`, `classification` (`Class I`, `Class II` or `Class III`), `center_classification_date` (`YYYYMMDD`), `status`, the recalling firm, an address, the distribution, the lot codes, the reason, the product description and an `openfda` block, among others; the connector reads three of them (the class stage of the download, below). |
| The download | One `download` makes several requests in order, and returns nothing until every one has succeeded, so a partial result never reaches the copy. **(1) The probe:** `GET …/device/recall.json?limit=1` (16 MiB, the limit of a join answer, since one product can carry millions of characters of lot numbers; `application/json`, 30 s, the host pinned to `api.fda.gov` over https after redirects). The answer must hold `meta.last_updated` as `YYYY-MM-DD` and `meta.results.total` above 10,000, otherwise it is not the dataset (`wrong_root`). The probe is what makes the 404 safe: openFDA answers 404 when a search has no match, so without it a moved or withdrawn endpoint would read as "no recalls" and the shrink check would be the only guard. **(1b) The probe of the enforcement reports:** `GET …/device/enforcement.json?limit=1`, with the limits of the first probe and the same test (a `meta.last_updated` day and a `meta.results.total` above 10,000, otherwise `wrong_root`); it makes the 404 of a class search safe in the same way, since a moved endpoint would otherwise read as "none of these is classified". A 404 of either probe itself, and any other failure of a request, is thrown as it is and fails the sync. **(2) The AI list:** the CSV of D2.17 (141 KB), unconditionally, with the limits, the host pin (`www.fda.gov`) and the header check of its own connector; the submission numbers are read from it. **(3) The composite validator:** the `last_modified` that the sync stores for this list is `recalls:<meta.last_updated>;enforcement:<meta.last_updated of the enforcement reports>;list:<the first 16 hexadecimal digits of the SHA-256 of the CSV text>`, and its `etag` is null (the column is TEXT, so the string is kept as it is). When it equals the stored one, the download ends as `unchanged` with no join request: the two probes and the AI list are the only requests of an unchanged day. Without it every daily run would download about 18 MB and spend 42 of the IP's 1,000 daily requests on openFDA (the two probes, 33 batches of the join and 7 of the class stage, on 2026-11-07); with it the join and the class stage run only when the recall dataset, the enforcement reports or the AI list changed (the recall dataset is updated weekly; the last update of the enforcement reports was `2026-10-28` when it was probed on 2026-11-10). A validator stored in the form of version 1, which has no enforcement day, never equals the new one, so the join runs again once and the records gain their class; the connector's `version` is 2. An `unchanged` run counts as a success, so the copy is stale only after 48 hours without one. **(4) The join:** `search=<field>:(<n1>+OR+<n2>…)&limit=1000`, in batches of 50 K numbers (`k_numbers`) and then of 30 PMA numbers (`pma_numbers`), each request 16 MiB, `application/json`, 60 s, the host pinned. An answer whose `meta.results.total` is above 1,000 is paged with `&skip=` in steps of 1,000, at most 5 pages, and more is `wrong_root`; a page that is not the last and holds fewer than 1,000 results is a search that did not page as it was asked, and fails the same way. A batch whose answer is cut at the limit (`too_large`) is split in half and asked again, down to 5 numbers, and past that the failure stands. A 404 on the first page of a batch is no match for that batch; a 404 on a later page is a search that did not page as it was asked, and fails the download as not the list (`wrong_root`), so that a part of a join never reaches the copy. Any other failure throws and fails the sync, a 429 with its `Retry-After` passed through. The job waits 500 ms between requests (about 120 a minute, half the limit), and a download is capped at 80 requests and 96 MiB of answers, those of the class stage included (past either: `too_large`). For today's list that is 32 batches of K numbers and one of PMA numbers, which with the two probes and the CSV is 36 requests before any paging or splitting, and the class stage adds one for each 50 of the products that the join keeps (7 of them, 43 in all, on 2026-11-07). **(4b) The class stage:** the distinct recall numbers of the joined products are sorted and asked of the enforcement endpoint, 50 to a request: `search=recall_number:(%22Z-1234-2020%22+OR+…)&limit=1000`, `application/json`, 16 MiB, 60 s, the host pinned to `api.fda.gov`, 500 ms apart like every other request. A 404 of a batch says that the reports hold none of its numbers, so they have no class. A recall has one report, so an answer whose `meta.results.total` is above 1,000 (one page), whose number of results is not its total, or that holds a result that is no object is not what was asked for and fails as not the list (`wrong_root`); no second page is ever asked. An answer cut at its limit is split in half as in the join, down to 5 numbers. Of a report three fields are read and two are kept: the class, which must be exactly `Class I`, `Class II` or `Class III`, and the day it was classified (`center_classification_date`: eight digits, `YYYYMMDD`, that form a day). A report of a number that the request did not hold is dropped and counted nowhere. Of two reports of one number the one that is kept is decided by their values and never by their place in the answer: the more serious class wins (a report with no class is the least), and of one class the earlier day (none is the latest), so that the same reports give the same class and day in whatever order openFDA lists them. The cap is shared with the join: the live list takes 36 requests before this stage, so 44 are left for it, which is about 2,200 joined products at most (fewer when a batch of the join pages or splits), and a list that outgrows the cap fails as `too_large` and gives no classes for a part of it (`MAX_REQUESTS`, 80, is the lever). **(5) The projection:** each answer is reduced at once to the fields of the record and the matched numbers, so `code_info` and the rest are dropped before the next request; products are deduplicated by `product_res_number`; the download returns one body, the canonical JSON of the dataset's `last_updated`, two counts (`skipped`, the products left out, and `classSkipped`, the recall numbers of which no report gave a class), the products sorted by recall number (each with its class and the day it was classified, or none) and the AI-list rows (submission number, device, company) that were joined, and its `bytes` is the sum of the answers. The day of the enforcement reports lives in the validator only. **(6) The parse:** the products are grouped by event; the root is `device/recall.json`, or none when the body is not that shape; the version of the copy is `meta.last_updated`; the shrink check (20 percent) applies to the events (155 on 2026-11-07, as the connector joins them); an empty join is `empty`; more than 5,000 events are not the list (`wrong_root`). A product whose recall number is not of the two forms, or whose initiation day is not a day, is left out and counted in `skipped`, with the tolerance of the CVE records (Q7): ten, or a hundredth of the rows when that is more, the rows being the products left out, the products and the rows of the AI list, each once. A report whose class is none of the three gives its product no class and counts in `classSkipped`, a count of its own because the product is kept and is one row, not two; it counts only when none of the reports of that recall number has a class, and `parse` adds it to what cannot be read without adding a row. A report with a class whose day is missing or is not a day keeps its class, has no day and counts as nothing: the record then says that the classification day is not stated. A stored body without a class or without `classSkipped` (a copy of version 1) is read as having none and counts nothing. |
| The join rules | From the AI list: `K` and six digits is searched as written, in `k_numbers`; `P` and six digits is searched as written, and `P`, six digits, `/S` and digits (a supplement) with the slash removed, both in `pma_numbers`, since the recall dataset writes a supplement without a slash (verified on 2026-11-07: the exact term of a supplement found 2 recalled products, while its base term found 40, the products of other supplements of the same PMA, and a base alone as the list writes it occurs too). The base of a supplement row is not searched. `DEN` and six digits (De Novo) is not searched, because a search of `k_numbers` for De Novo numbers found nothing (40 of the 1,614 rows are De Novo: a known gap). A recalled product is joined to a row only when one of its `k_numbers` or `pma_numbers` equals the row's searched term exactly. **Known limit:** a recall that cites only the base PMA number of an AI-listed supplement is not joined. |
| Licence | **CC0 1.0** (credit requested, not required; [license](https://open.fda.gov/license/)), **accepted on 2026-11-07 (Q34)**; the device enforcement reports, which are CC0 1.0 as well, were added on 2026-11-10 (Q37). openFDA's terms add nothing on caching or commercial reuse beyond CC0, apart from the GMDN content, which is licensed separately: the `openfda` block of a recall or of a report holds harmonized cross-references and is never read or stored, and the join uses the numbers of the product itself. The reports are read for the class of a recall and the day it was classified, and for nothing else: openFDA's documentation says that their data should not be used to track the lifecycle of a recall, and the FDA does not update the status of a recall after its classification, so the status is never read, and the record words the class as the FDA's classification and not as the state of the recall (Q37). openFDA's disclaimer (its results are unvalidated and are not to be relied on for decisions about medical care) is carried in every record by the closing sentence. The licence field reads `CC0 1.0 (https://open.fda.gov/license/): openFDA's device recall records and, for their class and classification day only, its device enforcement reports; the GMDN content of the openfda block is not used (Q34, Q37)`, and the attribution `FDA medical device recalls and enforcement reports through openFDA, US Food and Drug Administration (CC0 1.0), joined by VerifyWise to the FDA list of AI-enabled medical devices. Data provided by the U.S. Food and Drug Administration (https://open.fda.gov).` The second sentence of the attribution is the credit that openFDA asks for. |
| Record | One per recall event, not one per product (`ai_device_recall`, an **event**: the 24-month lookback of `ai_specific` applies, and `publishedAt` is the earliest `event_date_initiated` of its products, so a product with no readable initiation day is left out and counted). Per-product records would let one firm with 67 recalled products fill a run. The id is the event number (digits, 12 at most). The title is `FDA recall of an AI-enabled device: <recalling firm> (event <n>)`. The text begins `FDA medical device recalls (openFDA), joined to the FDA list of AI-enabled medical devices.` and goes on with the event number; the recalling firms (3 at most); the days initiated (the earliest of the products), posted (the earliest) and terminated (the latest, and only when the status is `Terminated`, that is when every product has ended); the status, the most open of the products (`Open, Classified` over `Completed` over `Terminated`); the root cause as the FDA records it (3 distinct values at most); the reason for the recall, that of the first product by recall number that has one (600 characters at most); the number of products with 3 descriptions of 200 characters at most; the devices on the FDA AI list that the recall covers (the device in 150 characters at most, its submission number and its company; 3 at most and how many more); the recall numbers (5 at most and how many more); the sentence of the class, `FDA classification, from its enforcement reports: <class>[ for k of the event's m products], classified <day>.`, where the class is the most serious that any product of the event has (Class I before II before III), k is how many products have it (left out when all do) and the day is the earliest on which any of the products that have that class was classified (when none of them has a day, `, classification day not stated` stands in place of `, classified <day>`), and, when no product of the event has a class, the sentence `The FDA's recall page shows the recall's class.`; and the closing sentence: `A recall is a firm's correction or removal of a device, classified by the FDA; it is not a finding that the device's AI caused the problem. openFDA says its results are unvalidated and are not to be relied on for decisions about medical care.` A cut ends in ` [shortened]`; the summary is at most 4,000 characters, enforced after it is assembled; the sentence of the class comes last before the closing one, so a text that has to be cut loses it first, and the class still shows in the identifiers. The address is the FDA's recall page of the event, `https://www.accessdata.fda.gov/scripts/cdrh/cfdocs/cfRES/res.cfm?start_search=1&event_id=<n>` (checked on 2026-11-07: HTTP 200, one table with a "Recall Class" column, read with a page reader). `identifiers`: the event number, the recalling firms, the recall numbers and the submission numbers (20 at most each), the status and, when a product of the event has one, the `class` (absent, and not null, otherwise). |
| Left out and text safety | **Never stored:** `code_info`, the addresses (`address_1`, `address_2`, city, state, postal code, country), `additional_info_contact`, `distribution_pattern`, `product_quantity`, `action` (the instructions to consignees), `firm_fei_number`, `cfres_id`, the `openfda` block, and the `k_numbers` and `pma_numbers` that are not the joined ones. They are dropped when each answer is projected, before the next request is made, so `code_info` never reaches the stored body. **From the enforcement reports** (Q37) only the class and the classification day are kept: the status (the FDA does not update it after classification), the recalling firm, the address, the distribution, the lot codes, the reason, the product description and the `openfda` block of a report are never read, and a report adds no number or name to a record. `reason_for_recall` and `product_description` are free text that a model reads: they are cleaned (zero-width and bidirectional control characters stripped, white space collapsed) and capped there, and go through the `<evidence>` escaping and the untrusted-data line. |
| Match | Names only, weak: `name:` keys of the recalling firm of every product of the event and of the company of every joined AI-list row, as written and without the legal form, 4 characters or more, at most 50 for a record. Both are keyed because the recalling firm may be a subsidiary or a distributor and not the company the FDA's list names. No key comes from the FEI number or from an address. The text names the firm and the AI list's company, and the prompt (`vr-14`) says that the recalling firm may be another company than the vendor and that a recall is not a finding that the AI failed. |
| Verdict | **Built, open (Q34); the FDA's class of the recall was added on 2026-11-10 by folding its device enforcement reports into this list (Q37), not as a list of its own.** An entry says that a device that the FDA lists as AI-enabled was recalled by a firm of that name; it never says that the AI failed (the FDA records a root cause, often a software one, but a recall is a correction of a device, not a finding about its AI). The join is only as good as the numbers it searches, hence the known limits above: De Novo numbers and the base-only citation of a supplement. |

### D2.21 Consolidated Canadian Autonomous Sanctions List

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Operator | Global Affairs Canada's consolidated list of the persons, entities and ships under Canada's autonomous sanctions regulations ([page](https://www.international.gc.ca/world-monde/international_relations-relations_internationales/sanctions/consolidated-consolide.aspx?lang=eng)). |
| Category · level | `legal_regulatory` · a sanctions list · vendor, by name: an entity that Canada lists. Class OFFICIAL, strength weak (D5.2). A state (`sanctions_entity`): no lookback, offered as of the copy. It raises a risk, worded as for the other sanctions lists (D4.13): "An entity named … is on …", at most one risk, Possible at most. At most 2 records go to a run. The coverage names the list even when a run found nothing (D4.10). |
| Endpoint, format | `https://www.international.gc.ca/world-monde/assets/office_docs/international_relations-relations_internationales/sanctions/sema-lmes.xml`, one static XML file. Observed on 2026-11-10: 2,918,344 bytes, `text/xml`, an `ETag` and a `Last-Modified` of 2026-11-09 11:14 UTC. 5,770 `record` elements under `data-set`: 3,551 individuals, 731 ships and 1,488 entities, told apart by the file's own elements. No total is stated in the file: the connector checks the floors of the next row. In the copy of 2026-11-10 the listing days run from 2008-09-04 to 2026-11-05. |
| Auth, limits | No key. At most 8 MiB, `text/xml` or `application/xml`, 120 s, a conditional GET on the stored `ETag` and `Last-Modified`. Answers must come from `www.international.gc.ca` under the directory of the address above, or the download fails as `fetch_failed`. The file is scanned by `vrdb/xmlScan.ts`, never built as a tree: the root must be `data-set`, a DOCTYPE with an internal subset is refused, and a scan error, fewer than 3,000 records, fewer than 1,000 entities or an earliest entity listing after 2010-12-31 fails as `wrong_root` (the two floors of the entities count every entity, one that no name makes a key for included); more than 20,000 records fails as `too_large`, and such a list is never read in part. |
| Cadence, increments | Listings come on a few days a month (in the copy of 2026-11-10: 4 listing days in the 90 days before and 12 in the 12 months before, 2 and 8 for the entities alone). Daily at 01:02 UTC (`2 1 * * *`); stale after 48 hours. No delta and no removal marker: an entity that the file no longer holds becomes `delisted`. |
| Licence | **Open Government Licence - Canada, through the dataset record; decided on delegation on 2026-11-10 (Q46).** The file has no license and the Canada.ca terms bar commercial redistribution unless specified otherwise; the open.canada.ca record `ab076f2e-94b1-4039-bb3d-58002deb826d` lists it under the OGL-Canada, which excludes personal information, and Q46 takes the record as the specification. Attribution: `Contains information licensed under the Open Government Licence - Canada (https://open.canada.ca/en/open-government-licence-canada). Source: Global Affairs Canada, Consolidated Canadian Autonomous Sanctions List. Not endorsed by the Government of Canada. Entries adapted by VerifyWise.` |
| Record | One per listed entity (`sanctions_entity`; `publishedAt` is the earliest listing day). The id is the compact name of the short name of the English name (the cleaned name before `" / "` and `(also known as`). It keeps up to 3 listings (regulation, schedule, item, day). On 2026-11-10: 1,486 records from the 5,770 records of the file, 0 unreadable: the 1,488 entity records less one that no name makes a key for (an English name of under 4 compact characters and no alias) and less one that shares its id with another (two names that differ only by a legal form are one record that holds both listings). An entity that no name makes a key for gets no record but counts for the floors. The listings are kept oldest first, in the order of the day, the regulation, the schedule and the item, so that no order of the file decides which stay. Text: `Consolidated Canadian Autonomous Sanctions List (international.gc.ca), a sanctions list.`, the entity, a sentence per listing, the other names (240 characters at most), then `Global Affairs Canada states that the list is for administrative purposes only, is not a regulation, has no force of law and may not be up to date; an entry names an entity, which may be another company of the same name. Contains information licensed under the Open Government Licence - Canada; adapted by VerifyWise.` At most 1,300 characters (median 560, maximum 784 on 2026-11-10): the title and the summary show the name cut at 150 characters and the other names cut at 240, and when the sentences are longer the youngest listings are left out one at a time (the third, then the second); the closing sentence is never cut. No id, key or identifier is made of a cut text. Address: the list's page with `&entity=<id>`. |
| Left out and text safety | **Never read:** a record with `GivenName-Prenom` or `LastName-NomDeFamille` (3,551 individuals), `ShipIMONumber-NumeroOMIDuNavire` (731 ships), or a birth or build date or a title or ship type, is skipped on the presence of the element (an empty one counts), before any other cell is read, not even its listing day; it is not copied and not counted as unreadable. The structure separates persons, so no name-shape rule is used. **Design decision:** a text that does not fit is never cut into an id, a key or an identifier. A primary name over 230 characters once folded, a regulation (its English part) over 100, a schedule over 20 as written, or a schedule cell longer than the kit reads, makes the record unreadable: it is counted, and more than the allowance fails the parse. An alias part, or the text of `(also known as …)`, over 120 characters once folded is no alias at all (a list of names with commas is such a part), and the record keeps its primary name and its other aliases (on 2026-11-10 no record has a key made of a cut text; 57 entity records of the file hold a part longer than 120 characters once folded, 55 in the alias element and 2 in the text of the parenthesis of the name). Aliases are split on a semicolon and a line break only, those with a Latin letter first, 8 at most, from the first 100 parts of the element. Names and aliases are cleaned (`lineOf`) and the record is written with `<` and `>` written as the angle quotation marks U+2039 and U+203A in the title, the summary and the identifiers, and the phrase "lookup note" written "lookup-note" in the title and the summary (`kit/record.ts`). |
| Match | Names only, weak: the whole name, its English part, the text inside `(also known as …)` and each alias (8 aliases and 12 names at most), as written and without the legal form, of 4 compact characters or more. The file has no website, LEI or number, so **a vendor that really is the listed entity gets Low and the name-only note** (D5.2). |
| Verdict | **Built, open (Q46).** One whole file with no individual or ship copied. An entry says that an entity of that name is listed under a Canadian regulation, never that the vendor is: found by name it gives Low support with the note. |

### D2.22 California Attorney General data breach list

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Operator | The California Department of Justice, Office of the Attorney General: the data security breach notices that organizations filed with it ([page](https://oag.ca.gov/privacy/databreach/list)). The named party is the filer, which can be a service provider acting for the breached business. |
| Category · level | `security_incidents` · a data breach notice list · vendor, by name: the organization that filed. Class OFFICIAL, strength weak (D5.2). An event (`breach_notice_summary`): the 24-month lookback applies, by the newest reported day. It raises a risk, worded in the analysis prompt "An entity named … reported a data breach to the California Attorney General": at most one risk, in `security_incidents`, Possible at most. At most 2 records go to a run. |
| Endpoint, format | `https://oag.ca.gov/privacy/databreach/list-export`, one CSV export of the whole list. Observed on 2026-11-10: 348,387 bytes, `text/csv; charset=utf-8`, an `ETag` and a `Last-Modified` of 2026-11-10 00:36 UTC. 5,425 data rows of 3 quoted columns (`Organization Name`, `Date(s) of Breach  (if known)` with two spaces, `Reported Date`), CRLF. `Reported Date` is `mm/dd/yyyy`, month first, so the shared `dayOf` is not used (it would misread 1,872 dates and null 3,369). Sorted newest first, 2026-11-09 back to 2012-01-20; no stated total. |
| Auth, limits | No key. At most 4 MiB, `text/csv`, `text/plain` or `application/csv`, 120 s, a conditional GET on the `ETag` and `Last-Modified`. Answers must come from `oag.ca.gov` under `/privacy/databreach/`, or the download fails as `fetch_failed`. Crawl-delay 10 s: one request a day keeps it. A row of other than 3 cells, a name over 200 characters or with no letter or digit, a breach-date cell longer than the kit reads, or a reported day that is not a real month-first day is unreadable (more than the allowance fails the parse); a header that is not the three names (white space and case ignored), a file that ends inside a quote, fewer than 4,000 rows or an earliest reported day after 2012-12-31 is not the list (`wrong_root`); more than 50,000 rows or a row of over 16 cells fails as `too_large`, and such a list is never read in part. |
| Cadence, increments | About 50 notices a month (602 rows reported in the 12 months before 2026-11-10). Daily at 07:12 UTC (`12 7 * * *`); stale after 48 hours. No delta and no removal marker: an organization that the file no longer holds becomes `withdrawn`. Only the reported day dates a record, since the breach-date cell is unreliable. |
| Licence | **Considered in the public domain; may be distributed or copied as permitted by law, per the conditions page; decided on delegation on 2026-11-10 (Q50).** The [conditions page](https://oag.ca.gov/conditions) excludes what the Department does not own, such as the sample notices that the breached organizations wrote, gives no warranty of accuracy and implies no endorsement. Only the Department's own list is copied (organization, breach dates, reported date); the sample notices are never fetched or linked, and person-named practices are not copied. Attribution: `Source: State of California Department of Justice, Office of the Attorney General, data security breach list. Not endorsed by the Department. Entries adapted by VerifyWise.` |
| Record | One per organization (`breach_notice_summary`; `publishedAt` is the newest reported day): rows grouped by the compact name of the short name of the spelling (the cleaned name once the persons in it are out). It keeps the count of its distinct (reported day, breach-date cell) pairs, the first and last reported day and the newest 3. On 2026-11-10: 4,372 records from 5,425 rows, holding 5,345 notices (at most 90 for one record, more than one for 567 records), 995 records with a newest reported day inside the 24 months before that day; 5 records have no key (their ids have 2 or 3 characters and every name of theirs has under 4 compact characters); 6 rows are unreadable (a name over 200 characters). Text: `California Attorney General data breach list (oag.ca.gov), a data breach notice list.`, the organization, the notices, then `The list names the organisation that filed the notice, which can be a service provider acting for the breached business; it does not say how many people were affected or what data, and it is not a finding of fault. Not endorsed by the Department; adapted by VerifyWise.` At most 1,300 characters (at most 808 with the caps of the fields, median 545 and maximum 772 on 2026-11-10; the title and the text show the organization cut at 120 characters), the closing sentence never cut. Address: the list's page with `?organisation=<id>`. |
| Left out and text safety | **Never stored:** a row whose name is a person's, dropped before anything is stored and not counted as unreadable: rule P (a leading `Dr.` or `Doctor`; a leading `Law Office(s) of`, `Law Firm of` or `Office(s) of`; a credential such as CPA, MD or DDS after the last comma or as the last word of a name of three words or more) and rule M (`First M. Last`, with an optional `Dr.` before it and one short suffix after it) (`californiaPracticeRule` and `CALIFORNIA_MIDDLE_INITIAL_NAME` in `kit/persons.ts`); the sample notices; every cell that is not read. **Design decision:** a person in a part of a name never stays in it. Each part is judged on its own before the name is used (the parts that an alias mark sets apart, the text inside each pair of parentheses and the text outside them); a part with the shape of a person is cut out with the mark or the parentheses that held it, and the spelling of the row is what remains, so that a sole proprietor's trade name is the record and its owner's name is not; the name as written, which holds the person, is never an id, a display, a title, a part of the text, an identifier or a key; a row with nothing left is dropped. An alias mark that opens a pair (`(dba First M. Last)`) is read off before the pair is judged. Brackets that do not pair up one to one (a pair inside a pair, a bracket never closed or never opened) cannot be taken apart: a person in the text between any two of them takes the whole part with it. **Design decision:** a name with a piece behind a comma and a blank, or an ampersand with a blank each side, that has the shape of a person, and in which no word is a legal form or a word of an organization (the kit's lists, from which the words that only join the parts of a name are left out), is a person's practice and is dropped; a family firm that carries a legal form or an organization word is kept (the cost of the rule), and a practice joined to a business by `dba` loses the business with it. Residuals: a comma or an ampersand with no blank, `and` and a hyphen join nothing, so a person behind one stays in the name; rule M reads ASCII letters and the whole name from its first letter, so a name with an accented letter, in lower case, behind `Mr.`, with a particle, last name first behind a comma or with a possessive is not caught; an alias mark written `d.b.a.`, `dba:`, `doing business as` or `t/a` splits nothing, so a person behind one stays in the name; a few plain two-word names of persons pass every rule and are shown only to a vendor of exactly that name. A name over 200 characters once cleaned (the longest is 255 characters, in 6 rows on 2026-11-10) is an unreadable row and is never cut into an id or a key; the title and the text show at most 120 characters of the name, cut and marked. The breach-date cell is not stored whole: of its first 21 dates the earliest and the latest that lie between 2000-01-01 and the reported day are kept, and the text of a notice shows them as `breach date(s) given` or `not stated`. Names are cleaned (`lineOf`) and the record is written with `<` and `>` written as the angle quotation marks U+2039 and U+203A in the title, the summary and the identifiers, and the phrase "lookup note" written "lookup-note" in the title and the summary (`kit/record.ts`). |
| Match | Names only, weak: the whole spelling (the name once the persons in it are out), each part split at `d/b/a`, `dba`, `a/k/a`, `aka`, `f/k/a`, `formerly` or `on behalf of`, and the text inside the first parentheses, as written and without the legal form, of 4 compact characters or more, 6 at most. No `domain:` or `regno:` key. **A vendor that really filed gets Low and the name-only note** (D5.2). |
| Verdict | **Built, open (Q50).** One whole sorted export of the Attorney General's own list. An entry says that an organization of that name filed a breach notice: never how many people or what data were affected, never fault, and not always that the filer itself was breached. |

### D2.23 UK OFSI financial sanctions enforcement notices

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Operator | The Office of Financial Sanctions Implementation (OFSI), HM Treasury, which publishes its penalty and disclosure notices on GOV.UK ([collection](https://www.gov.uk/government/collections/enforcement-of-financial-sanctions)). A notice is civil enforcement of a breach (the UK Sanctions List is D2.3). |
| Category · level | `legal_regulatory` · an enforcement or case list · vendor, by name: the party of a notice. Class OFFICIAL, strength weak (D5.2). Its paragraph of the analysis prompt is the one for enforcement and case lists, not the sanctions one: "An entity named … appears in …", at most one risk, Possible at most, and a notice is not a designation. An event (`sanctions_penalty_notice`): the 36-month lookback applies, by the newest notice day. At most 1 record goes to a run. |
| Endpoint, format | The GOV.UK Search API: `https://www.gov.uk/api/search.json` with `filter_organisations=office-of-financial-sanctions-implementation`, `count=1000`, `order=-public_timestamp`, 8 fields and `start`. Observed on 2026-11-10: one request, 140,591 bytes, `application/json`, no `ETag` or `Last-Modified`; `total` 249 with 249 distinct links. 19 are of type `notice` (15 monetary penalties, 3 disclosure notices, 1 joint statement with no party); the other 230 are dropped in the download. |
| Auth, limits | No key. Each page 4 MiB, `application/json`, 60 s; at most 2 pages, 400 ms apart, through the shared pager `govukSearchPages`; a first answer that needs more pages is not the list (`wrong_root`). Answers must come from `www.gov.uk` under `/api/`, or the download fails as `fetch_failed`. The reader returns null (`wrong_root`) unless every page states the same `total` and the distinct links equal it (a row that shifts across a page edge fails the sync), and unless 12 to 2,000 parties are read (the list holds 19 and does not shrink: a parser that broke would give few). A notice is unreadable when its title opens as a penalty or a disclosure but no party can be read from the slots of the Record row; when its title is longer than 300 characters once cleaned or ends in the cut mark, or the description of a date-titled disclosure is longer than 1,000 characters or ends in it; when a party holds `<` or `>`, has no letter, begins with a date or with `and`, begins or ends with a comma, is nothing but legal forms, ends in the cut mark or is longer than 150 characters; when the key of a party is longer than 120 characters or the notice names more than 25 parties; or, for a notice that names a party, when its link is no clean page under `/government/publications/` (200 characters at most) or its `public_timestamp` is no time with a zone. The unreadable notices are counted, nothing of one is kept, and more than the allowance fails the parse. |
| Cadence, increments | The API sends no validator, so the job downloads the list every week, Wednesday at 10:18 UTC (`18 10 * * 3`), and the hash of the body tells an unchanged copy; stale after 192 hours. A party that a later copy lacks becomes `withdrawn`. A notice's day is the Europe/London day of `public_timestamp` (the clock is never read, so the same list gives the same records on any day). The version of a copy is the newest notice day of its records (2026-09-02 on 2026-11-10). |
| Licence | **Open Government Licence v3.0; decided on delegation on 2026-11-10 (Q52, with the CMA and MHRA lists), in the way of the UK Sanctions List (D2.3).** Only the GOV.UK search metadata is read; the OGL excludes personal data, so a person-shaped name is dropped. Attribution: `UK Office of Financial Sanctions Implementation (HM Treasury) enforcement notices, published on GOV.UK. Contains public sector information licensed under the Open Government Licence v3.0. Adapted by VerifyWise; no endorsement by HM Treasury is implied.` |
| Record | One per party (`sanctions_penalty_notice`; `publishedAt` is the newest notice day): 19 records on 2026-11-10 from 20 names (19 parties and 1 name that the person rule drops), 1 notice each; 14 have a newest notice day inside the 36 months before that day; 0 unreadable. The id is `ofsi:` and the compact name of the short name of the party (so that `Example Bank PLC` and `Example Bank Limited` are one party), 120 characters at most: a longer key makes its notice unreadable and is never cut. The party is read from a fixed slot, never from the slug or from the kind that a link suggests: the title after `Imposition of monetary penalty` or `Imposition of a monetary penalty` and a colon, an en dash or a hyphen; the title after `Disclosure notice` and one of them; or, for the 2 date-titled disclosures (`Disclosure notice: 08 September 2025`), the sentence of the description that begins `Disclosure notice of an offence under financial sanctions by`, then optionally `charities`, up to a bracket alias or a spaced dash, split at a comma and at the word `and` (one of the 2 lists 3 parties). A trailing bracket alias (`(EBL)`) is cut from every party, and `London Branch` stays. A title of another kind (the joint statement) names no party and is no failure. It keeps the count and the newest 3 notices (path, title, kind, day). A notice that names a person next to a party shows its kind alone as its title and keeps no path (GOV.UK makes the end of a page's address from its title, which may hold the name), and a record whose newest notice is such a notice shows the collection page of the list as its address. Text: `UK OFSI financial sanctions enforcement notices (gov.uk), an enforcement or case list.`, then `A notice of the Office of Financial Sanctions Implementation names the party of a monetary penalty or of a published disclosure for a breach of UK financial sanctions; it is not a designation, and the party is not on a sanctions list because of it.`, the sentence `Party as written`, the count and the newest notices (title, kind, day) and `Adapted by VerifyWise from GOV.UK under the Open Government Licence v3.0.` At most 1,300 characters (median 562, maximum 628 on 2026-11-10). The title is `OFSI notice: ` and the party; the identifiers are `party` (whole), `notices` and `newest`. Address `https://www.gov.uk` and the path of the newest notice, or the collection page (17 distinct addresses among the 19 records on 2026-11-10: the 3 parties of one notice share its page, and no notice of the real page names a person next to a party). |
| Left out and text safety | **Never stored:** the 230 documents that are not notices; the `format`, `display_type` and `first_published_at` of every document; the description of every notice but a date-titled disclosure, and of that one all that follows the party list; the link of a notice that names a person next to a party; a party of fewer than four letters and digits even with its legal form, which no key could match (it is dropped with no trace, as a person is). **Natural persons:** OFSI's own page says that personal data is excluded (Q52), so a party that has the shape of a person's name is dropped with no trace in the body (a notice that names only such a party gives no record and is no unreadable notice). The rule is the connector's own and shape-based, and its closed vocabularies are named in the header comment of the connector. A party of two to five words, each a name word or a one-letter initial (at least one that is no initial), is a person's name; titles (Mr, Mrs, Dr, Prof, Rt Hon, Capt, Col, Fr, Sr and others), suffixes (Jr, III, Esq, PhD, `the younger`, and a suffix that a comma sets apart, such as `, MBE`) and particles (de, van, bin, al, abu, ben and others) count among the words. A role phrase around a name (`its director Casey Sample`, `Casey Sample, owner`, `et al`) is put aside and the rest is read, and quotes round a word or round a whole part are taken off before the test. Never a person: a party with a legal form (the forms that end a company's name as its last word included: Corporation, Incorporated, Corp, NV, SARL, Oy, KK, Kft, ASA, OOO, JSC, PJSC, AB, LP, Sdn Bhd) or with a word of an organization (Bank, Group, Trust, Services and others), a party introduced by `charities`, a name with a digit or a symbol, and a name in a script that has no case. A person next to a company is dropped and the company is kept (the notice then shows its kind alone as its title and keeps no path): in a title that is a list (split at a comma, `and`, `&`, a slash, a semicolon, `with`, `and/or`, `together with`, `as well as`, `plus`, `c/o`, `vs` and `versus`), in the party list of a description, round a trade-name marker (`t/a`, `trading as`, `d/b/a`, `a/k/a`, `f/k/a`, `formerly`, `also known as`, `now known as` and others), after a linker (`owned by`, `operated by`, `managed by`, `run by`, `on behalf of`, `acting for`) and after a legal form or a word of an organization that a possessive follows (`Example Ltd's director Casey Sample`). The rule drops a real company now and then (one with no legal form and no word of an organization whose words look like a name; on 2026-11-10 one, a 2022 notice), a weakness that is accepted because a person must never be a key. **Not read** (a person's name can pass in these shapes, so the rule is not complete): a person named first in a title's list stays in one party (`Casey Sample & Example Ltd`, with any joiner); a joiner that the list above does not name (`or`, `for`, `via`, `through`, `+` and others), a joiner with no blank around it (`Example Ltd&Casey Sample`) and a spaced dash between a company and a person join them in a way that nothing tells apart from one name; a person's possessive before a company (`Casey Sample's Example Ltd`); a name of six words or more, counted with its titles, suffixes and particles; a name of initials alone (`J.R. K.L.`); a person whose last word is one of the last-word forms (`Casey Sample AB`) or who is followed by a tail of forms after a comma (`Casey Sample, co`), read as a company and kept; two quotes in a row and a quote inside a word; a bracket left open before another bracket (`Example Ltd (Casey Sample (EBL)`) and a bracket whose inside begins with `and` (`Example Ltd (and director Casey Sample)`), which stay in the party with the person; a role word after a title (`Ms. director Casey Sample`) and a role that begins with a capital after a comma (`Casey Sample, Director`); a name written in lower case (a title keeps it, alone or in a list; a description leaves it out of its list as a role), a name with the last name first, a name of one word, and a joint title (`Mr and Mrs Casey Sample`); and the address of a notice's page, which is GOV.UK's and keeps its path when the notice is retitled, so a notice first published under a person's name and retitled to a company keeps an address that holds the name, and the company's record shows it (a check of the address against the current title was measured and not shipped: only 8 of the 19 real addresses are the slug of their title, so it would hide the page of plain notices). **Also not read**, with no person passing but a wrong party stored: a role in a bracket after a person (`Casey Sample (director)` drops the person and stores `director` as a party), a pair of quotes that a comma splits (`Example Ltd, "Casey Sample, owner"` drops the person and stores `owner"`), and `et al` after a company (`Example Ltd et al`, stored whole). A company whose only form is one that the person rule does not list (a bare `Co`, `AS`, `SAS`, `Pty`, `Srl` and others that the keys take off a name), and one that ends in a dotted form that no list names (`S.R.L.`), are read as a person's name and dropped (a person with such a surname is kept out too). In the real page of 2026-11-10 no party text has one of these shapes: the 3 parties that hold a joiner (a comma, an ampersand or another) have no side with the shape of a person's name, none holds `or`, `for`, `via`, `through`, `with` or `+`, none starts with a title, none has one word, and the 3 parties of six words or more hold a legal form; 3 titles and 10 descriptions of the notices hold a spaced dash, and it splits nothing. Two party texts of two words do have the shape of a person's name, and they are kept only because their disclosure introduces them with `charities`. Cells are cleaned (`lineOf`) and the record is written with `<` and `>` written as the angle quotation marks U+2039 and U+203A in the title, the summary and the identifiers, and the phrase "lookup note" written "lookup-note" in the title and the summary (`kit/record.ts`). |
| Match | Names only, weak: the spellings of the party (5 at most, those of the newest notice first), each as the notice writes it without its trailing bracket alias and each without its legal form too, of 4 compact characters or more. No `regno:`, `domain:` or `lei:` key. On 2026-11-10 the 19 records carry 30 keys (8 records have 1 key, 11 have 2), all of them name keys. **A vendor that really is the party gets Low and the name-only note** (D5.2). |
| Verdict | **Built weak, open (Q52).** The whole list is proven in one request (249 documents; the 19 notices equal an aggregate query's), but the party exists only in text and the notices were not reconciled with the collection page; it is kept because it is small and exact today (20 of 20 names read on 2026-11-10 are organizations, and the person rule drops one of them, a real company). The person rule is shape-based with closed vocabularies; a person-shaped name outside them is a documented residual (see Left out and text safety) with no occurrence in the real page today. An entry says that OFSI published a penalty or disclosure notice that names a party of that name, never that the party is designated. |

### D2.24 AI Hallucination Cases database

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Operator | Damien Charlotin (HEC Paris), the database's author ([page](https://www.damiencharlotin.com/hallucinations/)). It lists court decisions that addressed false or fabricated content in a filing, with the AI tool that a decision names. |
| Category · level | `ai_specific` · an AI incident list · vendor, by name: the AI tool that a decision names, a product and not its maker. Class TRUSTED_PRESS (a scholar's compilation), strength weak (D5.2). An event (`hallucination_cases`): the 24-month lookback applies, by the newest decision day. It raises a risk: the analysis prompt's paragraph for AI incident lists words it "An entity named … appears in …", at most one risk, in `ai_specific`, Possible at most. Researched only for a vendor with a website. At most 2 records go to a run (a vendor with two tools as aliases sees both). |
| Endpoint, format | `https://www.damiencharlotin.com/hallucinations/hallucinations/download.csv`, a CSV built for each request. Observed on 2026-11-10: 2,192,398 bytes, `text/csv`, no `ETag`, a `Last-Modified` equal to the response time. 2,286 records of 19 cells equal the site's counter. `case_id` has holes (656 missing) but its highest value is present. |
| Auth, limits | No key. At most 16 MiB, `text/csv` only (a Cloudflare challenge arrives as `text/html` and fails the sync), 120 s; answers must come from `www.damiencharlotin.com` under `/hallucinations/`, or the download fails as `fetch_failed`. No usable validator: the 2.2 MB is downloaded every day and the hash tells an unchanged list. The header is read by name (7 columns are used: `State(s)`, `Date`, `Party(ies)`, `AI Tool`, `Alleged`, `Vendor Disputed` and `Vendor Disputed Explanation`; a missing one is not the list, an extra one is fine); fewer than 1,500 rows, or more than 5,000 tools, is not the list; more than 20,000 rows or a row of over 128 cells fails as `too_large`. A row of another width than the header, with a `Date` that is not exactly YYYY-MM-DD and real, with a tool cell over 200 characters once cleaned or longer than the kit reads, or with an `Alleged` or `Vendor Disputed` that is neither Yes nor No on a row that names a tool (a flag cell longer than the kit reads is none; a row that names no tool is never asked about its flags), is unreadable: it is counted, and more than the kit's allowance (ten, or a hundredth, and at most a tenth of the entities) fails the parse. |
| Cadence, increments | Daily at 15:25 UTC (`25 15 * * *`); stale after 48 hours; the version is the newest decision day of a counted decision. Counts by year are anchored on the year of the newest decision of the file, never on the clock, so the same file gives the same hash on any day. A tool key that a later export lacks becomes `withdrawn`. |
| Licence | **CC BY 4.0, stated on the page and in the export's headers; decided on delegation on 2026-11-10 (Q39), for the owner's confirmation.** The site's robots.txt has a Content-Signal `ai-train=no`: the data never trains or fine-tunes a model, and the analysis model reads only per-tool counts at run time. If the owner reverses Q39, a gate is added and the copy is deleted. Attribution: `AI Hallucination Cases Database, Damien Charlotin, https://www.damiencharlotin.com/hallucinations/. Licensed under CC BY 4.0 (https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/). Counted and adapted by VerifyWise; not endorsed by its author.` |
| Record | One per tool key (`hallucination_cases`; `publishedAt` is the newest decision day): 83 on 2026-11-10, from 238 of the 2,286 rows (2,040 name no tool, 5 name only a generic phrase or a token of under 4 letters and digits, and 3 that name a tool are marked `Alleged`, which counts nowhere); 0 unreadable. A row counts for each distinct key it names. The key is read from the free-text `AI Tool` cell: the cell names no tool when it is empty or begins, in any case, with `implied`, `unidentified` or `unknown`; otherwise it is split at `,` `;` `/` `+` `&` and the words `and` and `or`; in each part the parentheticals are cut (an unclosed `(` to the end), double quotes become blanks, a leading `and`, `or` or `with` and a possessive `'s` go; a generic phrase (`AI`, `Gen AI`, `Internet searches`, `Google Search`, `Other generative AI platforms`, `Other generative AI tools`, `Premium Westlaw subscription`) or a token of fewer than 4 letters and digits once its legal form is off gives no key. The key, which is also the id, is the compact name of the short name of the token, so a changed spelling in the source is a new key (one withdrawal and one insert). A record holds the count, the first and last decision day, the counts of the newest year of the file and the 3 years before it, counts by filer (lawyers, self-represented litigants, others), the 3 countries with the most decisions, and the number of decisions that a vendor disputed (`Vendor Disputed` is Yes and the explanation, read as a compact text, holds the tool's key or holds a word of 5 letters or more that is inside the key). Text: `AI Hallucination Cases database (damiencharlotin.com), an AI incident list.`, then `Each entry is a court decision that addressed false or fabricated content in a filing and names the AI tool used; the database notes that naming a tool does not show that the tool caused the error, and an entry is not a finding about the tool's maker.`, the counts and `A decision that names several tools counts for each. Counted by VerifyWise from the published CSV; AI Hallucination Cases Database, Damien Charlotin, CC BY 4.0.` At most 1,300 characters (median 642, maximum 762 on 2026-11-10), the closing sentence never cut. Address: `https://www.damiencharlotin.com/hallucinations/?tool=<id>`. |
| Left out and text safety | **Never stored:** `Case Name` (natural persons in at least 57 % of the rows), `Pointer`, `Source`, `Details` and `Hallucination Items` (free text that quotes decisions and names persons), and every column that is not read: the 7 columns that are read are named in the row above, and the others are dropped before any row is built. `Party(ies)` is read only for its first words (the role of the filer) and `Vendor Disputed Explanation`, which is vendor-written, only to tell which tools it names; neither is kept. No party, lawyer, court or case text is stored; the only natural person is the operator, named in the attribution as the license requires. **Design decision:** no person rule is applied to the tool cell, because the column holds the names of AI tools (the distinct values of the real export were read in full when the list was built: no person); a tool cell that named a person would become a key that matches only a vendor of exactly that name, and the tool cell is the first place to look if the list ever starts to carry developers' names. A tool cell, or a flag cell, that is longer than the kit reads makes the row unreadable and is never cut into a key; an explanation of more than 300 characters once cleaned (or longer than the kit reads) names no tool, because a cut fragment must never decide a dispute, and its row still counts. The title and the text show the first spelling of a tool cut at 60 characters; the identifier `tool` and the names hold the whole spelling (at most 200 characters), and no identifier is a cut text. A `State(s)` cell of more than 200 characters is cut before it is split at `;`, so its last piece can show under the countries as a label that ends in the cut marker: a display only, never a key or an id (the longest real cell has 28 characters). Cells are cleaned (`lineOf`) and the record is written with `<` and `>` written as the angle quotation marks U+2039 and U+203A in the title, the summary and the identifiers, and the phrase "lookup note" written "lookup-note" in the title and the summary (`kit/record.ts`). |
| Match | Names only, weak, and a product is not its maker: the key's distinct spellings (5 at most), as written and without the legal form, of 4 compact characters or more. A vendor matches only when its own name or an alias is the tool's name; no alias table is added. |
| Verdict | **Built weak, open (Q39).** The export is complete (the counts equal the site's) and licensed, but the only evidence for a vendor is a free-text cell, 89 % of the rows name no tool, and the keys are products. An entry says that a court decision named a tool of that name, never that the tool or its maker erred (most entries concern a filer's misuse). |

### D2.25 FTC civil penalty cases

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Operator | The US Federal Trade Commission's table of consumer protection civil penalty orders, published as one workbook that its data catalog points to ([catalog](https://www.ftc.gov/data.json)). |
| Category · level | `legal_regulatory` · an enforcement or case list · vendor, by name: the defendant company of an order. Class OFFICIAL, strength weak (D5.2). An event (`civil_penalty_summary`): the 36-month lookback applies, by the newest order day. It raises a risk, in the analysis prompt's paragraph for enforcement and case lists: "An entity named … appears in …", at most one risk, Possible at most; an order is not a finding about the vendor. At most 2 records go to a run. |
| Endpoint, format | Two files on `www.ftc.gov`. The catalog `https://www.ftc.gov/data.json` names the workbook of the dataset `FTC Bureau of Consumer Protection Penalty Actions`, whose address carries the fiscal year: today `https://www.ftc.gov/system/files/ftc_gov/documents/civil_penalty_cases_fy2000-2026.xlsx`. Observed on 2026-11-10: 33,658 bytes, no `ETag`, `Last-Modified` 2026-10-16 13:37 UTC. One sheet, `civil_penalty_cases_fy-2000-202`, 6 columns (`Case Number`, `Case Name`, `Office`, `Ordered Amount`, `Date of Order`, `Basis`; the sheet writes a blank before the fourth name), 391 data rows dated 1999-10-04 to 2026-08-13, and a footer that states 391 cases and an amount sum of 7,432,595,610.31, both equal to the rows. |
| Auth, limits | No key. Catalog: at most 1 MiB, `application/json`, 60 s, answered at exactly the pathname `/data.json` (a redirect to another page of the host fails as `fetch_failed`). Workbook: at most 4 MiB, 120 s, the spreadsheet type or `application/octet-stream`, under `/system/files/ftc_gov/documents/`, requested only at the address that the catalog gives and only when it names one, 5 s after the catalog (its Crawl-delay, `kit/pause.ts`). An answer from anywhere else fails as `fetch_failed`. The catalog must hold exactly one dataset titled `FTC Bureau of Consumer Protection Penalty Actions` with exactly one distribution whose address is `https://www.ftc.gov/system/files/ftc_gov/documents/civil_penalty_cases_fy2000-20NN.xlsx` with NN of 26 or more (a file of an earlier year is no copy of the current list); anything else is `wrong_root`. No conditional GET: neither request's validators would tell whether the list changed, so the hash of the body tells an unchanged list. Not the list (`wrong_root`): a workbook without the sheet `civil_penalty_cases_fy-2000-202`, of the 1904 date system or with a malformed sheet; a header that is not the six names in order (blanks at the ends of a cell are ignored); a footer (the last row that holds anything) that holds more than the count of the cases in its second cell and their sum in its fourth, or whose count or sum differs from the rows (the sum is compared to the cent over the amounts that are numbers, the unreadable rows included); anything but blank rows between the last case and the footer; fewer than 300 cases or an earliest order after 2000-12-31. A sheet of more than 5,000 rows or 16 columns, or a workbook past the zip limits (64 entries, 4 MiB an entry, 8 MiB in all), fails as `too_large`. A row is unreadable when a cell after the sixth holds anything, its case number does not begin with a letter and four to seven digits that no further digit follows (more text may follow the number: 2 rows of the file have some), its case name is empty or longer than 300 characters once cleaned (it is never cut), its amount is neither a dash nor a plain number of dollars (an empty cell is neither), or its day is not a date serial; the unreadable rows are counted, and more than the allowance fails the parse. |
| Cadence, increments | 7 to 25 orders a year (calendar years 2000 to 2025; 363 distinct case number and day pairs in the 391 rows on 2026-11-10, and the rows number 8 to 25 a year); the workbook changed on 2026-10-16 and the catalog's record is dated 2026-09-15, so the catalog lags. Weekly, Tuesday at 04:55 UTC (`55 4 * * 2`); stale after 192 hours. The version of a copy is its newest order day (2026-08-13 on 2026-11-10). A defendant that a later copy lacks becomes `withdrawn`. A renamed workbook is followed through the catalog; if the catalog lags and the old address is gone, the sync fails visibly. |
| Licence | **CC0 1.0, as the catalog states for the dataset.** The FTC website policy asks for attribution where feasible, implies no endorsement and does not allow the FTC seal. Names of natural persons are not copied. Attribution: `Source: United States Federal Trade Commission, www.ftc.gov (consumer protection civil penalty cases, CC0 1.0). Not endorsed by the FTC. Entries adapted by VerifyWise.` |
| Record | One per defendant company (`civil_penalty_summary`; `publishedAt` is the newest order day). The id is the compact name of the short name of the party (the party without its legal form). **Design decision:** a compact name of up to 100 characters is the id itself, and a longer one gives its first 83 characters, a tilde and the first 16 hex digits of the SHA-256 of the whole compact name, so that two long names with one start are two records and no order of the rows changes an id (no compact name holds a tilde; the longest id of the file of 2026-11-10 has 54 characters, and none holds a tilde). A party under 4 letters and digits once its legal form is off is no entity. A party is a piece of the case name between slashes and semicolons (the alias marks open the alias parties), so a name that joins companies with `and`, `&`, `+` or a comma is one party and one record under the joined name (7 of the 293 records have `and`, `&` or `+` in a spelling on 2026-11-10). A company's orders are its distinct (case number, order day) pairs, and an order's amount is the largest among its rows, never their sum (the rows of an order repeat or split the amount); a row that names two spellings of one company counts once for it. It keeps the order count, the first and last year, the ordered total (the amounts of its orders added, in whole dollars; an order with a dash for the amount adds nothing) and the newest 3 orders. On 2026-11-10: 293 companies and records from the 391 rows (total 391, 0 unreadable), at most 4 orders for one (279 companies have 1 order, 10 have 2, 2 have 3 and 2 have 4), 31 companies whose newest order day is inside the 36 months before that day; 29 of the 391 rows give a dash for the amount.
