**Answered on 2026-10-29:** every recommendation of Q1–Q24 is accepted. **Q25–Q27**, raised by M0's final review, were answered on 2026-10-30 with their recommendations; you asked for them to be taken as given. **Decided on 2026-10-31:** Q4 (named: `tech@verifywise.ai`), Q5, Q6 (the UN gate stays closed), Q7, Q8, Q9, Q21, Q22 and Q28; see their rows. **Decided on 2026-11-05:** Q30 to Q32, and the build of Q29 behind its closed gate; see their rows. **Decided on 2026-11-07:** Q33 and Q34, and the build of both lists, at the product owner's request for every keyless list that could be added right away; see their rows. **Decided on 2026-11-10 (delegated):** Q35 to Q53, and the build of 23 lists, the fold of the FDA's device enforcement reports into the recalls and the decision not to build eight lists (D2.20 to D2.44), at the product owner's request for every list of the research of 2026-11-09 that can be added at once; see their rows. Two answers are still open: the permission of Q29 (the operator's written confirmation, or your written acceptance of the risk), and a name for the owner of Q18 (you, until you name another), which now covers the `sr-6` entries too. Four more items are open and are not part of the round of 2026-11-10: the UN list behind the gate Q6, the load of the CVE baseline, the certificate that the HHS OIG list waits for, and the append-only mirror mode (open items 4, 8, 9 and 10 below; the two answers above are items 7 and 11). Two decisions of 2026-11-10 wait for your confirmation: Q39 and Q42 (open item 6), and the eight lists built weak are on by default (open item 12).

**Open items for the product owner after 2026-11-10:**

1. Q33 and Q34 were accepted under the delegation, in the way of Q5 and Q31. Reverse either if you disagree: the copy is then deleted and a gate added.
2. A vendor that really is on the Entity List shows as Low with the name-only note (D5.2). That is by design, since the lists of Commerce and State carry no identifier or website. Confirm that you are content with the label meaning "an official list, identity not proven by a key".
3. The untyped entries of the Consolidated Screening List include people (mostly on the Denied Persons List, US persons), stored with a name and a country only. Say if you want them dropped by a heuristic instead.
4. SEC enforcement and the FTC AI cases need an append-only mirror mode, a change of the sync engine (D2.18). Decide whether you want that as a piece of work of its own.
5. Other governments' sanctions lists (Australia and Switzerland, among others) were not looked at (D2.18); Canada's was built on 2026-11-10 (D2.21, Q46). Say if you want a round for the others.
6. Q39 (the Content-Signal `ai-train=no` of the AI Hallucination Cases database) and Q42 (the Dutch register's reuse grant, which names no licence) were accepted under the delegation and are built open. Confirm them, or reverse either: a gate is then added and its copy is deleted.
7. Q29: the AI Incident Database is built and tested behind the closed gate (`AIID_PERMISSION_GRANTED = false`; D2.14, D4.12). It opens only on the written confirmation of its operator, the Responsible AI Collaborative, that a commercial product may store the weekly export and show records derived from it under CC BY-SA 4.0, or on your written acceptance of that risk. Until then the list is never downloaded, scheduled or looked up.
8. Q6: the UN Security Council Consolidated List is built and was checked against the real file, behind the closed gate (`UN_PERMISSION_GRANTED = false`; D2.5). The UN's terms allow personal, non-commercial use only, and its written permission is not sought, since the UK and EU lists carry its designations with the UN reference number. Say if you want the permission requested.
9. The HHS OIG List of Excluded Individuals and Entities (LEIE) waits for a certificate: `oig.hhs.gov` sends an incomplete certificate chain, so the server's TLS cannot connect (D2.44). The licence is not the obstacle. Decide whether the deployments add the intermediate certificate to their trust store; the list can be built after that.
10. The CVE baseline: the gate Q7 is open, but each deployment loads the baseline (648,475,703 bytes and 417,486 records on 2026-10-31) once, by hand, with `scripts/vrdbCveBaseline.ts`, and nothing downloads it. Until it is loaded, the scheduled sync of the CVE list is refused as `no_baseline` (D2.6).
11. Q18: no owner is named for the yearly review of the registry, which covers the six `sr-6` entries too (reviewed on 2026-11-05; the hygiene test lists them as due from 2027-11-05 and fails on them from 2028-05-05). The lists of 2026-11-10 add no entry, so `sr-6` is unchanged.
12. Eight lists of 2026-11-10 are built weak and on by default (D4.14): `uk_atrs`, `uk_cma`, `uk_ofsi`, `uk_mhra`, `piperoll`, `charlotin`, `nl_algoritmeregister` and `ftc_competition`. Their names are read out of free text or matched by name alone, and a wrong match costs one Low risk that tells the reader to check the identity (`uk_atrs` and `nl_algoritmeregister` are context only: a wrong match of theirs is a wrong entry of context and raises no risk). Say if you want any of them turned off.

### Risks

- **A. Sanctions name matches.** Naming a company as sanctioned when the entry is another company is the largest exposure. Mitigations: entities only; name-only matches are Low with the note; the text says "an entity named … is on …", never "the vendor is sanctioned"; a person must accept a finding before it reaches the register; the list's date is shown; delistings are tracked; no automatic notification. OFAC itself does not confirm potential matches and says many are false positives ([FAQ topic](https://ofac.treasury.gov/faqs/topic/1591)). The Consolidated Screening List (D2.19) adds lists whose listing is no sanction: the text names the list, the prompt says that a listing on the Entity List or the Military End User List is a licence requirement (`vr-14`), and each record ends with the sentence that the list is an aid to screening and that its official publication is to be checked.
- **B. Personal data.** The UK, EU, UN and Canadian files include individuals (the UK file has birth dates and passport numbers; the Canadian file held 3,551 individuals and 731 ships on 2026-11-10, D2.21); parsing drops them before anything is stored, and the OGL does not license personal data anyway. The lists of Commerce and State in the Consolidated Screening List are the exception: they carry no entry type, so every entry is kept, individuals included (mostly on the Denied Persons List); a record stores a name and a country only, and never an address, a birth date, a citizenship, a passport or a national ID number (D2.19), and the product owner may ask for a heuristic that drops them (the open items after Q34). The lists of 2026-11-10 that name parties in free text keep natural persons out of their records by the means of D4.14 (risk O).
- **C. Registry maintenance.** 109 entries to review yearly, among them 48 of the 79 publishers and 6 of the 20 OFFICIAL entries that still wait for a readable page; every change is a pull request and a new `REGISTRY_VERSION`.
- **D. Rate limits and cost.** Every deployment downloads the lists itself: KEV 1.8 MB on change, OFAC about 7 MB (`SDN.CSV` and `ALT.CSV`, R39), UK 22 MB of XML, EU about 26 MB. With `ETag`, `Last-Modified` and the hash, most syncs end as `unchanged`. The NVD's keyless limit (5 per 30 s) is per client, so several workers must share one spacer (Redis, M2). The SEC allows 10 per second; we use far less. The Consolidated Screening List is one JSON file of 34 MB that ITA regenerates daily, so most of the six-hourly checks end as a 304. The recalls are the costly case: openFDA has no field projection, and a join downloads about 18 MB of answers in about 35 requests, against 1,000 requests a day for one IP address; since 2026-11-10 a class stage adds one request for each 50 joined products (43 requests in all on 2026-11-07). The composite validator limits the join to the days on which openFDA's recall dataset, its enforcement reports or the AI list changed, and a download is capped at 80 requests and 96 MiB, the class stage included, which leaves room for about 2,200 joined products (D2.20). The 22 mirrors of 2026-11-10 add as many downloads: the largest are the two NHTSA zips, which inflate to 390.6 MB and 312.8 MB and are read by streaming (D4.7), most syncs still end as `unchanged`, and every connector pauses between its own requests (D4.14).
- **E. Storage.** Row counts were not measured, since no list was downloaded; the UN list has 274 entities, and the file sizes suggest the entities of all four sanctions lists stay small. A CVE mirror is the large one, hence the subset of Q7.
- **F. Format changes.** The UK list gained three file types and a new format guide at the end of 2025; the KEV schema changes a few times a year; OFAC's CSV has no header and its file names differ from its labels; cvelistV5's asset names are irregular, and it has had three days without an end-of-day release since 2025 (2025-01-13, 2026-06-20 and 2026-07-19), which the connector reads from the day's last hourly release. Connectors validate what they parse and fail the sync rather than delist; every format has fixture tests.
- **G. The NVD covers less.** Since 15 April 2026 most new CVEs are not enriched, so they have no CPE data and `virtualMatchString` misses them. This already affects today's lookup (R34). The answer is matching on the CVE records' `affected` vendor and product (M2), since the NVD's own `affected` lists cannot be filtered.
- **H. Excluded by the keyless rule:** SAM.gov, the NVD with a key, the FTC API, the World Bank's internal endpoint (D2.12), and the aggregators of §2.3.
- **I. What the eval cannot see.** Replay cases with `.example` hosts do not test whether the registry is right about real publishers; the golden set does.
- **J. Shared state.** `vrdb_lookups` shows which vendor keys were looked up, without the organisation; it is read only by the server.
- **K. Positive claims on trust pages** get High under rule V (Q12).
- **L. Registers that match strongly.** FedRAMP (an official list, strong on a verified domain) and GLEIF (trusted press, strong on a stored LEI) are context only but can match strongly, and the credibility rules would have read a finding that cites one as High (an official record) or Medium. The ten context-only lists (`CONTEXT_ONLY_RECORD_SOURCES`) are never a supporting source, in the rules and in both sentence builders, so that a finding that cites only a register is Low (D4.14).
- **M. False matches of the lists that are on by default.** Every new raising list is found by name alone, carries the lookup note and gives Low support, and the prompt allows one risk for an entry, Possible at most, worded "appears in" (`vr-15`). The riskiest are PipeRoll (unverified; of its 36 name keys on 2026-11-10, 10 are codenames, a language, a wiki, a city chatbot or an institute, none a common word or a person; a title word of a person's shape is never a name, a fail-closed cost for a company whose only name is in a title) and the FTC nonmerger list (parties cut from matter titles; most of a sample of 30 right): both stay on, since a wrong match costs one Low risk that tells the reader to check the identity. A list that keys a brand or a product (the AI Hallucination Cases database, the Foundation Model Transparency Index, Epoch AI) misses a vendor that is stored under a longer name, which is the safe direction.
- **N. The size of the pack and of the briefing.** At their caps the records can reach 78 and about 161.5 KB against the 100,000 bytes of the pack, so the registers are given up first (D4.14). The briefing of the research loop would cost about 5 KB a step for 37 lists, so a list added on 2026-11-10, but the sanctions list of Canada, is named there only when it gave records or failed; the stored coverage of a run holds the attribution of every active list (about 9 KB a run), which is storage only.
- **O. Rules measured on today's files.** The four rules that tell a person from an organization (FTC penalties, the Commission's cases, the Colombian sanctions, the California breach list) and the free-text readers of the GOV.UK lists (supplier, party, notice and manufacturer) were measured on the copies of 2026-11-10, as were the rules that stay in the connectors (the academic titles of the Dutch register, the title shapes of PipeRoll, the terms of a person's business in the C2PA list); the shapes that a rule does not see are stated as residuals in the sections of the lists that have them (D4.14). A new row can slip a person through, and a new title shape loses a row but does not produce a wrong name; the shrink check and the checks of each connector fail a sync that a broken parser would empty.
- **P. Lists that stop without failing.** The legacy data file of FedRAMP may freeze, which only its newest status day shows; PipeRoll (one editor) and the AI Hallucination Cases database (one author) can change their rules or vanish, and a vanished file fails the sync and withdraws nothing; the Commission's case files are objects that expire after 30 days unless the Commission rewrites them, and a vanished object is a failed sync, stale after 192 hours, never an empty list; and every GOV.UK list depends on the Search API answering `count=1000` (the documented maximum of 1,500 was never tested). A real loss of more than a fifth of a list's active records is refused as `shrank` at every scheduled sync, so the copy goes stale until an operator steps in (on the smallest lists a loss of 4 records of `uk_ofsi`'s 19 or 3 of `fmti`'s 11), and a weekly list whose sync fails after its three attempts, is refused or is told to wait (`Retry-After`) is asked again only a week later, so its copy is stale from the eighth day after its last good sync until the next one.
- **Q. Terms that bind.** Q39 and Q42 are built open on the delegation and wait for your confirmation (the fallback is a gate and the deletion of the copy). GLEIF's terms carry liquidated damages of CHF 100,000 per case of implying an association with GLEIF, so the attribution and the second sentence of every record say that none exists (Q53).
- **R. Large files with no header row.** The two NHTSA files are 390.6 MB and 312.8 MB inflated (a connector that inflated one whole would cost about 0.7 GB) and have no header row, so they are streamed and read by column position; a row-shape rule and floors on investigations, campaigns and entities catch a column that moves (D4.7, D4.14).

---

## D8. Phase 2 milestones

No commit, push or pull request without your explicit permission at any milestone.

- **M0. Rules in code** (no database, no UI). The registry v0 (D3) with the answers to Q8–Q15, the classifier, the credibility function and their unit tests; the eval's types and scorer; the updates of the existing cases; new replay cases 22–27 and 31–33 with a fake VRDB answer; prompt `vr-7` and the schema without `confidence` behind `VENDOR_RESEARCH_VRDB`. Gate: the Servers unit suite, the eval ratchet, `format-check` in Servers and Clients, and a scratch tsconfig typecheck of `services/` (the project's tsc does not check it).
- **M1. MVP, behind the flag.** The migrations (the shared `vrdb_*` tables, `vendor_vrdb_matches`, the findings' `credibility`, `credibility_basis` and source fields, the runs' `coverage` and `registry_version`); the KEV, OFAC and UK connectors, and the EU connector if Q2 is yes, with their sync jobs, schedulers, retries and staleness; the VRDB lookup in place of the per-run KEV and OFAC lookups (NVD, SEC, HIBP and the status page unchanged); credibility end to end (API, chip, rationale, note); the source badges; the coverage block; the strings (server en, de, fr where needed; client de, fr, es); the isolation registry entry, test and audit entry; replay cases 28–30; the docs and the user guide. Gate: both `format-check`s, the Servers build and unit suite, the integration shards, the Clients typecheck, build and vitest, the audit, a browser smoke on throwaway services, the golden-set run with the flag off and on, Q5 and Q9 answered, and your approval to turn the flag on by default.
- **M2. Expansion.** The NVD read-through (a shared Redis spacer) and the SEC read-through (after Q3 and Q4); the CVE mirror (after Q7: `REJECTED`, vendor and product); the status job that relabels pending findings citing a rejected, withdrawn or delisted record; the UN list (built and checked against the real file; Q6 keeps it behind its closed gate, since the UN's terms allow personal, non-commercial use only); the unknown-host script; vendor identifiers (Q1).
- **M3. Contract.** The backfill (D4.6), dropping `confidence`, removing the old path (the per-run KEV, NVD, SEC and OFAC lookups, `sourceTiers.ts`, `confidenceCeiling`) and the flag; re-checking the watched sources (FTC datasets, the HHS export, the EDPB feed, and the EU AI Act database after 2 December 2027). **Done on this branch (2026-10-31 and after):** the backfill, the re-check of the watched sources (Q22: none meets D2's bar, nothing built) and Part B1, which removed the old path and the flag; the database is always on, and the run's own SEC lookup is gone with the others. **B2**, which drops the `confidence` column, is on `feature/vendor-finding-drop-confidence` for release N+1. The gates Q4 and Q7 are open and Q6 stays closed; Q29, added on 2026-11-05 (M4), is closed too.
- **M4. AI-specific sources (2026-11-05, Q29 to Q32).** The connectors of MITRE ATLAS, the NHTSA crash reports and the FDA list, and of the AI Incident Database behind the closed gate Q29 (with a reader of its Excel export on the zip reader and the XML scanner), their definitions, schedules, record types and classes (D2.14 to D2.17, D4.12); registry `sr-6` (D2.18, D3.4); prompt `vr-13`; the rule that a weak record is no publisher for Medium (D5.3); and the client's names and attributions for the four ids. No migration.
- **M5. Two more keyless lists (2026-11-07, Q33 and Q34).** The connectors of the US Consolidated Screening List (one JSON file that `download` projects before the sync hashes it) and of the FDA recalls of AI-enabled devices (a probe, the AI list, a composite validator and a batched join), their definitions, schedules, record types and classes (D2.19, D2.20, D4.13); prompt `vr-14`; the client's names and attributions for the two ids; and the user guide. No migration, no gate and no registry change.
- **M6. The third round of lists (2026-11-10, Q35 to Q53).** The connectors of 22 mirrors and of the read-through of GLEIF, on one kit (the connector factory, the readers, the person rules and the pause helper), their definitions, schedules, record types and classes (D2.21 to D2.43, D4.14); the FDA's recall class from its enforcement reports, folded into the recalls (D2.20, Q37); prompt `vr-15`; the rule that a record of a register is context and never a supporting source, with its own line in the credibility sentence of the server and the client; the order of the evidence pack and the coverage rule for the new lists; the client's names and attributions for the 23 ids; and the user guide. Eight lists were not built (D2.44), and the HHS OIG list waits for a certificate (open item 9 of D7). No migration, no gate and no registry change.

- **Checks after writing (2026-10-29):** every one of the 212 addresses in this document was requested again (a header request, else a 200-byte range). None of the pages answered 404; 15 did not answer 200 to our reader: 403 from 9 (the S entries, openPR and the UN's terms), 429 rate limits from 3 (Article 71 was read again afterwards), 406 from 1 (time.com) and 2 timeouts; the bare OFAC export path answers 404 because it is a prefix, not a page. The claims that rested on one page were read again (OFAC FAQs 88 and 1031 and Recent Actions, the UK guidance and format guide, the SEC's API page, the NVD FAQ and key page, the CVE FAQ, the EDPB register, the FTC library, the HHS portal, CNIL, The Record, CISA's guidance, Article 71), and four sentences were corrected: the October OFAC dates, the UK format change, the EDPB example id and ruling R36. The code facts of D1 (paths, lines, list sizes 16, 43 and 81) and the replay (21 cases, 0 regressions) were checked against the branch.
- **Re-checked on 2026-10-31 (M3):** the watched sources of Q22 and the EDPB feed, read again on the operators' own pages; the list files were probed by header requests only, and none was downloaded.
  - **FTC:** the [data sets](https://www.ftc.gov/policy-notices/open-government/data-sets) page now lists four enforcement datasets, covering fiscal years from 1996 (2000 for the last) into 2026: the nonmerger enforcement actions, the merger enforcement actions and the civil penalty actions of the competition mission, as CSV, and the civil penalty actions of the consumer protection mission, labelled CSV but an Excel workbook. No cadence is stated, and the page as read today names no fields, so the four fields recorded on 2026-10-29 are UNVERIFIED until the data dictionaries are read. Observed: with the bot's User-Agent the four files answer a header request with 200 (with curl's default one, 403), 9 KB to 101 KB, `Last-Modified` 18 September 2026 for the three of competition and 16 October 2026 for the one of consumer protection. The [Legal Library](https://www.ftc.gov/legal-library/browse/cases-proceedings) still lists 6,124 items, Commissioner statements among them, so not all are cases; its titles name companies, and it filters by topic ("Privacy and Security", with "Data Security"), but it offers no feed or export of cases (the foot of the page could not be read, so an export link there is UNVERIFIED). The [RSS feeds](https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/stay-connected/ftc-rss-feeds) carry press releases, blogs and others, none of cases; the [API](https://www.ftc.gov/developer) still covers Do Not Call complaints and HSR early-termination notices only, with an api.data.gov key; the [website policy](https://www.ftc.gov/policy-notices/website-policy) still makes most material a work of the US Government in the public domain, asking for attribution where feasible and no implied endorsement. Change: the four datasets and their coverage; otherwise no change.
  - **HHS OCR:** the [breach portal](https://ocrportal.hhs.gov/ocr/breach/breach_report_hip.jsf) lists 765 breaches under investigation (761 on 2026-10-29) in nine columns, none of them an id; its Excel, PDF, CSV and XML exports are still actions of the page's script, with no stable address, and so is its archive tab; it asks for no key or account; the portal shows no terms, and hhs.gov answered 403 again (UNVERIFIED). It names the covered entities, persons among them. Change: the count; otherwise no change.
  - **EDPB:** the [register](https://www.edpb.europa.eu/our-work-tools/consistency-findings/register-for-article-60-final-decisions_en) still has no controller field and no structured export, one PDF per decision; it filters by authority, legal reference, topic, outcome and date (filters the 2026-10-29 record did not mention); a current id is `EDPBI:EE:OSS:D:2026:4265`. The [feed](https://www.edpb.europa.eu/feed/news_en), the only one the [national news](https://www.edpb.europa.eu/news/national-news_en) page links, is RSS 2.0 titled "EDPB News" and mixes EDPB and national items (nine of its latest ten were national); most titles name a company, and some descriptions add a "Controller:" line in their text, not as a field. Observed: 200, `application/rss+xml`, `ETag`, `Last-Modified`. The page says the national items are not EDPB communications: they are the national authorities' own news, an authority's word all the same, so `edpb.europa.eu` stays OFFICIAL under the `europa.eu` rule. [Reuse](https://www.edpb.europa.eu/copyright_en) is still authorized for commercial and non-commercial purposes, with the source acknowledged. No change.
  - **Result:** none of them meets D2's bar, so all stay watched and nothing is built. The FTC's consumer protection file comes closest (official, keyless, public domain, 33 KB); whether it holds privacy and data-security cases with matter numbers and company names can be settled only by reading it, a list-file download that waits for your permission. The EU AI Act database stays on the human list: check it again, not before 2 December 2027. **Tried again on 2026-10-31 (Phase 2):** the FTC's consumer protection file (`civil_penalty_cases_fy2000-2026.xlsx`), its dictionary and the data sets page answered 403 to header requests with a plain user agent, and nothing was done to get past the refusal, so the file's columns, its row count and whether it holds privacy and data-security cases are still unverified. Q22 was decided on that basis: keep watching, build nothing.
- **AI-specific sources (read on 2026-11-04 and 2026-11-05, Q30):**
  - **AIID:** the [snapshots page](https://incidentdatabase.ai/research/snapshots/) (the link of the weekly export), the [terms of use](https://incidentdatabase.ai/terms-of-use/) (effective 2025-08-07: the CC BY-SA 4.0 collections and the exclusions, the clause on commercial exploitation, the bar on high-volume bot access, the operator's contact) and [an incident page](https://incidentdatabase.ai/cite/1/). The real files of the four lists were downloaded into a scratch directory, for structure and counts only. Observed: the export of 2026-11-02 is 2,611,619 bytes, with `ETag` and `Last-Modified`, and its sheet `Incidents` holds 1,784 incidents with ids up to 1797. On 2026-11-05 the page served 14 links to exports as plain HTML, all on the bucket host, the newest dated 2026-11-02.
  - **ATLAS:** [atlas-data](https://github.com/mitre-atlas/atlas-data) (README, [LICENSE](https://github.com/mitre-atlas/atlas-data/blob/main/LICENSE)), `dist/manifest.yaml` and `dist/v6/ATLAS-2026.10.yaml` (format 6.1.0, 77 case studies, 27 of them incidents and 50 exercises); the review process for contributions was not read. The atlas.mitre.org page of a case study answers a plain request with 404. The root of the `main` branch holds `LICENSE` and `README.md` and no NOTICE file.
  - **NHTSA:** the Standing General Order [page](https://www.nhtsa.gov/laws-regulations/standing-general-order-crash-reporting) (the data may be incomplete or unverified; personal and confidential business information is redacted) and the three current CSV files. **UNVERIFIED:** that the page is at this address (the decision of 2026-11-05 marked it to be checked). Observed: each of the three files carries an `ETag` and `Last-Modified` 2026-10-15, and through the page guard with the bot's User-Agent the three answer 200.
  - **FDA:** the [list page](https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/software-medical-device-samd/artificial-intelligence-enabled-medical-devices) (the public-domain statement; **UNVERIFIED** at this address, as the NHTSA page) and its CSV (1,614 rows, `Last-Modified` 2026-09-04, no `ETag`; through the page guard with the bot's User-Agent it answers 200, and a request with `If-Modified-Since` answers 304).
  - **Not built:** the AIAAIC [user guide](https://www.aiaaic.org/aiaaic-repository/user-guide) (its terms page needs a Google sign-in and could not be read), the OECD AIM [methodology](https://oecd.ai/en/incidents-methodology), the AVID [terms](https://avidml.org/legal/terms-and-conditions) and the [MIT AI Incident Tracker](https://airisk.mit.edu/ai-incident-tracker); the registry entries of `sr-6` cite them. For GW DAIL and the Agent Incident Registry no export and no licence were found.
- **Two more keyless lists (read on 2026-11-07, Q33 and Q34):**
  - **CSL:** the [page](https://www.trade.gov/consolidated-screening-list) (the three whole-list downloads and their addresses; updated automatically every day at 5:00 AM EST/EDT; an aid to screening, to be checked against the official publication), ITA's developer terms on developer.trade.gov (no licence named; a service may search, display and analyze the data; the notice that a service should show; no modifying the content while naming ITA as its source) and the [endorsement disclaimer](https://www.trade.gov/endorsement-disclaimer) (ITA's own links and use of trade names; no restriction), the last two read with a page reader. The real file was downloaded once into a scratch directory, for structure and counts only. Observed: 34,186,586 bytes with `ETag`, `Last-Modified` and `Cache-Control: no-store`; a HEAD request 404, a GET 200, a range GET 206, `If-None-Match` and `If-Modified-Since` 304; the `ETag` and `Last-Modified` unchanged after 1.5 hours; 26,265 entries over 12 lists, with 13 rows in `sources_used`; through the page guard with the bot's User-Agent the host answers 200.
  - **openFDA:** the [documentation of the recall endpoint](https://open.fda.gov/apis/device/recall/), [authentication](https://open.fda.gov/apis/authentication/) (240 requests a minute, 1,000 a day), the [license](https://open.fda.gov/license/) and the terms (nothing on caching or commercial reuse beyond CC0, the GMDN content aside), and the recall page of an event (`res.cfm?start_search=1&event_id=<n>`: HTTP 200, one table with a "Recall Class" column), read with a page reader. Observed: `meta.last_updated` 2026-11-05 and 59,703 recalled products; `limit=1000` accepted; searches of `k_numbers` and `pma_numbers`, and a 404 for a search with no match; the exact term of a supplement found 2 recalled products and its base term 40; the form of `product_res_number`, `res_event_number`, the dates and `recall_status` over 400 products of four eras; the join of the probe, 26 requests and 18,451,173 bytes; through the page guard with the bot's User-Agent the host answers 200. **UNVERIFIED:** whether openFDA holds De Novo numbers in another field than `k_numbers` (a search of that field found none), and the documented limit of `skip` (25,000), which the join does not approach. **Added on 2026-11-10 (Q37):** the [documentation of the enforcement endpoint](https://open.fda.gov/apis/device/enforcement/) (`recall_number`, `classification`, `center_classification_date`). Observed: `meta.last_updated` 2026-10-28 and 40,450 reports; a search of 50 quoted recall numbers answered 200 with 50 reports (569,938 bytes), all of them with a class (Class I 5, Class II 45) and a classification day; a search that finds nothing answered 404 with `NOT_FOUND`; `Cache-Control: no-cache, no-store, must-revalidate`.
  - **Not built:** the SEC's two RSS feeds (25 items each), the FTC's list of cases and its `robots.txt` (`Crawl-delay: 5`), the CourtListener API, the ICO's enforcement listing and the CFPB's complaints, as far as the facts of D2.18 go; the number of listing pages of the SEC's older releases was not checked.
